| T195 | **Is sequential presentation a cost paid against joint training?** | **Yes — this is the premise of the whole continual-learning literature.** Gradient descent on `L_B` treats parameters load-bearing for A as free, so every family in [[wiki/concepts/continual-learning.md]] exists to recover something of what simultaneous access would have given, and the best outcomes reported are *parity* with joint training rather than an improvement | **Not always.** Loading 16 mixed patterns (alternating integer-periodic and parametric-family) into one reservoir subspace-by-subspace gave mean NRMSE **0.094** against **0.19** for loading all 16 simultaneously with scaling and regularisation tuned for the latter. Jaeger states the result is surprising and unexplained. The two homogeneous conditions came out near-parity in the ordinary direction (0.078 vs 0.063 integer-periodic; 0.136 vs 0.131 parametric), so the inversion appears exactly where the pattern set is **heterogeneous** ([[wiki/entities/conceptor.md]]) | Decides whether incremental writing is a constraint to be mitigated or a regulariser to be exploited. The proposed mechanism is visible in the same source's diagnostics: under simultaneous loading a single solution strikes a compromise across all items on equal terms, while under incremental loading item `j` competes only with items `1…j−1` and can be fitted sharply in the space they left — which predicts the advantage should grow with heterogeneity and vanish with homogeneity, which is what was observed | `LIVE` — one source, one architecture, `N = 100`, 16 items, and the incremental condition required an extra hand-set step (singular values pushed through `tanh(50(2σ−1))`) that the simultaneous condition did not, so the comparison is not clean. The discriminating experiment is cheap and unrun: sweep pattern-set heterogeneity with the rectangularisation applied in both conditions |
| T196 | **Should a compound's meaning depend only on its immediate children, or on the whole structure it sits in?** | **Local (strong / first-level) composition.** Meaning is built bottom-up per node, so any sub-expression is substitutable by its own value without changing the result — `14 − (2+3)` = `14 − 5`. Every program-induction and parse-tree architecture in the wiki assumes it: [[wiki/concepts/program-induction.md]]'s straight-line compositions, [[wiki/entities/bayesian-program-learning.md]]'s part/sub-part hierarchy, [[wiki/entities/neo-neural-theorizer.md]]'s executor applied to intermediate states — and it is what makes an intermediate node an *estimate of something* rather than a hidden activation (G10). Hupkes et al. 2020 make it testable: unroll the expression against the model and compare | **Global (weak) composition.** Meaning follows from the *total* structure plus the atoms, so a compound can mean different things inside different wholes. Carnap's case: any two tautologies `X`, `Y` are truth-conditionally identical, yet `Peter thinks that X` and `Peter thinks that Y` need not agree — the local reading forces a false identity, the global one does not. The source calls the localist account "highly controversial" for natural language, and the three architectures tested behave globally (localism consistency 0.46 / 0.59 / 0.54): they learn *chunks of adjacent functions* and apply them at once, which the authors decline to call a mistake — "imagine computing `5 + 3 − 3`" ([[wiki/entities/pcfg-set.md]]) | Decides whether a low localism score is a defect to fix or a strategy to permit, and therefore whether the wiki's per-node composition assumption is a requirement or a modelling convenience. Concretely: if global composition is admissible, then G10's "unreliable intermediate nodes" is partly a category error — the model was never obliged to have them — and the wiki's cheapest structure probe (ask the model to parse, or to evaluate a sub-expression) loses its warrant. If local composition is required, then chunking is exactly the shortcut that must be trained out, and PCFG SET's systematicity drop (0.92 → 0.72) is its measured cost | `LIVE` — and the two positions are separable by an experiment nobody has run. The observed chunking has a *diagnosed* cause that is independent of the local/global question: applying a function to an over-length argument fails, so unrolling breaks for a reason having nothing to do with which composition regime is correct. Re-run the localism test with the training argument-length distribution wide enough that unrolling never produces an out-of-range argument; if consistency stays low, the model genuinely composes globally, and if it rises, the whole result was G70/G69 in disguise |
| T197 | **Is un-flagged retrieval error a defect to engineer away or a phenomenon to reproduce?** | **Defect.** The wiki's retrieval mechanisms are scored normatively: [[wiki/entities/neuromatch.md]]'s 49% precision at 97.9 AUROC is recorded as the reason G37 is only `PARTIAL`, and the first missing piece named there is a *certificate* that would let an approximate matcher flag its own errors. On this reading a first stage that discards relational structure is a tractability compromise to be removed as soon as something cheap and structure-sensitive exists (Plate 1993, T185) | **Phenomenon.** MAC/FAC states **fallibility** as one of four explicit design criteria ([[wiki/entities/macfac.md]]): "being simple, these models will not always produce the best match ... while this might be a disadvantage in a normative model, it could be an advantage in modelling human similarity-based access". The argument against smart indexing is that it "can predict superhuman access behavior", and the model is validated by *ordinal fit to human error rates* (LS ≈ SF ≫ AN ≈ FOR), beating ARCS — a full constraint-satisfaction network — which got the ordering wrong in two places | Decides what a reasoning model's retrieval stage is being optimised for. If fallibility is a defect, spend the certificate and the extra structure at stage 1 and accept superhuman access. If it is load-bearing — the "primacy of the mundane" argument is that surface matches are usually the *right* guides to action, and a system that always retrieved the deepest structural analog would be wrong most of the time — then a retrieval stage tuned to maximise structural recall is optimising the wrong quantity, and the missing instrument is a measure of *how often the best structural match is the useful one*, which no source in the wiki reports | `LIVE` — the two literatures never argue with each other because they never share a metric; MAC/FAC is scored against human proportions and NeuroMatch against ground-truth containment |
| T198 | **Does locality buy resistance to catastrophic forgetting?** | **Yes.** Backpropagation moves every weight in service of one global output loss, which is the mechanism by which prior structure is overwritten; predictive coding's weights each descend only their own layer's residual, so learning a new example cannot recruit the whole network — locality is offered as a forgetting-resistance argument in itself (talk-nd-brain-learning-algorithm, **(tentative)**) | **Not established, and in tension with the framework's own headline result.** A clamped predictive-coding network's error nodes settle on the backpropagated `δ_l` to a close approximation (Whittington & Bogacz 2017, 2019) — if the approximation is close, the weight trajectory is close, and the forgetting behaviour should be close too. No continual-learning benchmark comparing the two is cited | Decides whether a builder gets continual learning free by choosing a local objective, or still has to pay for it with gating, replay or code separation ([[wiki/concepts/continual-learning.md]]) | `LIVE` — resolvable cheaply: run clamped predictive coding and backpropagation on the same task sequence and measure backward transfer. The interesting quantity is whether forgetting tracks the *tightness* of the backpropagation approximation |
| T199 | **Is relational retrieval bought by building indices, or by making the encoding uniform?** | **Indices.** The case-based reasoning tradition retrieves the structurally right case by indexing memory on themes, principles and goals — the vocabulary of the index carries higher-order abstractions, and retrieval is a lookup in it. This is what makes a large case library usable, and it is the assumption behind every schema-retrieval mechanism in the wiki that presumes a candidate set ([[wiki/architectural-gaps.md]] G37) | **Uniform encoding, and indices are then unnecessary.** MAC/FAC's index is *computed from* the representation (a count over the functors the item was encoded with), so it updates automatically whenever encoding changes. Relational retrieval rises with expertise and with intensive encoding, and the proposed cause of both is a **uniform relational vocabulary** — a comprehensive domain theory makes everyone's episodes share higher-order predicates, so relational overlap becomes surface overlap in the index. With enough domain structure the behaviour converges on a richly indexed case-based reasoner with no indexing machinery built. The failure route confirms it: Bassok's verb-interpretation effect ties relational encodings idiosyncratically to surface content, and relational access collapses (Forbus, Gentner & Law 1995, [[wiki/entities/macfac.md]], [[wiki/concepts/schema-assimilation.md]]) | Decides where effort goes in a schema-retrieval system: into a retrieval structure over a fixed encoder, or into canonicalising the encoder. Position B also predicts that indexing psychology is *superhuman* — a well-indexed memory would retrieve the best structural match, which people demonstrably do not | `LEANING B` — B explains the expert/novice and encoding-depth data with no extra machinery and generates the falsifiable prediction (uniformity, not index construction, is the causal variable); but the two accounts have never been separated experimentally, and B has only been demonstrated at 18–36 hand-encoded items, which is exactly the scale at which an index is unnecessary anyway |
| T200 | **Is systematicity a property of the architecture or of the training distribution?** | **Architecture.** Hupkes et al. 2020 score three standard sequence architectures on PCFG SET — data generated *by* the intended compositional rule, with argument strings never repeated so memorisation is priced out — and get systematicity 0.53 / 0.56 / 0.72 against task accuracies of 0.79 / 0.85 / 0.92, with the differences tracking architectural properties (the Transformer's order-independence, ConvS2S's local composition operator). On this reading the seq2seq family lacks the mechanism, and a compositionally-generated corpus cannot supply it ([[wiki/entities/pcfg-set.md]], G16) | **The training distribution.** Lake & Baroni 2023 hold the architecture class fixed — a plain 3-layer seq2seq transformer, 1.4M parameters, no new components — and change only how the signal arrives: 100,000 episodes, each a fresh latent interpretation grammar inferable only from 14 in-context study examples. Result: **100%** exact-match systematic generalization, against **0% in 10/10 runs** for the same architecture trained on the same examples as a static corpus, and ≤0.22% / 0.87% error on SCAN's and COGS's lexical splits ([[wiki/entities/mlc.md]]) | Decides where the wiki spends effort. Position A puts compositionality in the architecture slot, where most of [[wiki/architectural-gaps.md]] already sits; position B puts it in the objective slot (G30), which is nearly empty and cheap to act on. The partial reconciliation is that neither result contradicts the other — they differ in *presentation*, not in architecture or in data-generating rule — but the reconciliation costs G16 its strongest phrasing, since the intended rule is unidentifiable from a corpus and identifiable from a task stream. What keeps it `LIVE` is that the win is facet-selective: productivity is 0.30–0.50 under A and **exactly 0.000** under B wherever the episode sampler did not vary length, so B has not shown the architecture is unlimited, only that one facet is purchasable | `LIVE` |
| T201 | **Is human compositional behaviour a rule system with lapses, or an approximator carrying rule-shaped biases?** | **A rule system with lapses.** The probabilistic symbolic model infers the gold interpretation grammar from the study examples and translates queries by it; every non-algebraic response is a lapse drawn uniformly, with one fitted lapse rate. This is the position every program-induction and language-of-thought account in the wiki assumes ([[wiki/concepts/program-induction.md]], [[wiki/concepts/language-of-thought.md]]), and it is not obviously wrong here — people do hit 80.7% exact algebraic match, and 72.5% on outputs longer than any studied | **An approximator with structured biases.** Human errors are not uniform: one-to-one translation accounts for 24.4% of them, iconic concatenation for 23.3% of function-3 errors, and in a no-example probe 62.1% / 79.3% / 93.1% of participants follow one-to-one, iconic concatenation and mutual exclusivity. A gold-grammar oracle must call all of that arbitrary, and loses to MLC on log-likelihood of human responses; augmenting the oracle with the same three biases ties MLC on the few-shot task and still loses by ≥57 nats on the open-ended one, because a symbolic model that hard-codes three biases cannot fit the fourth thing people do ([[wiki/entities/mlc.md]]) | Decides what a brain-inspired reasoner is supposed to reproduce. If B, then a perfectly systematic module is the *wrong* target and the biases are content to be modelled rather than noise to be suppressed — and the wiki gains a mechanism for supplying them (sample them into `p(T)` at a measured rate) that costs no architecture. The caveat is symmetric: MLC was *trained on* an 80/20 algebraic/heuristic mixture calibrated to the human accuracy it is then scored against, so its bias fit is partly fitted rather than predicted, and the Supplementary Information reports it failing on bias nuances it was not optimized for — 98.0% mutual exclusivity against people's 68.2%, iconic concatenation collapsing to 66.7% against 93.9% (T202) | `LIVE` |
| T202 | **Does installing an inductive bias by sampling it into `p(T)` give you the bias, or only its frequency?** | **The bias.** MLC's episode sampler pairs 80% of queries with the algebraic output and 20% with human-like heuristics, and the resulting frozen model reproduces all three measured human biases at close to the human rate on the open-ended task — one-to-one 66.0% (people 62.1%), iconic concatenation 85.0% (79.3%), mutual exclusivity 99.0% (93.1%), modal all-three pattern 65.0% (58.6%) — with nothing written into the architecture, and it acquires ME in the direction *opposite* to the neural-network default (Lake & Baroni 2023, [[wiki/entities/mlc.md]]) | **Only its frequency, in the context it was measured in.** Probed on contextual factors the sampler never varied, the same frozen model applies ME absolutely — 98.0% of samples against 68.2% of human responses — while people grade it by the number of counter-examples (`β = 1.76, p < 0.001`) and by the size of the response pool (`β = 2.05, p < 0.01`), plausibly by pragmatic inference about what the task is asking; and its iconic concatenation *drops* to 66.7% (people 93.9%) on mappings that violate one-to-one. Re-optimizing the same architecture on those participants recovers the human profile (68.6% / 93.8%), but only after the response pool was added to the input as an extra study example — the model previously had no channel to condition on it (Supplementary Information SI-2) | Decides whether the wiki's cheapest route to installing a prior ([[wiki/concepts/core-knowledge.md]], third route) scales to priors that must be *applied conditionally*. If B, a sampled prior is a marginal statistic and every context the bias should respond to must itself be varied in `p(T)` **and** be visible in the input — so the sampler inherits the coverage problem of G66 one level down, at the level of the bias's arguments rather than the task's. The constructive reading is the authors' own: a meta-learner "must be optimized for the kinds of generalizations it will be asked to make", which makes bias-context enumeration a design step nothing currently audits | `LEANING B` — the two positions are measurements of the same model in different settings, and only B's setting is out-of-sample; A's fit is partly calibration (the 80/20 ratio was set to the human accuracy it is scored against) |
| T203 | **Is abstract format a hippocampal specialisation, or a shared property of the fronto-hippocampal system?** | **Hippocampal.** Courellis et al. 2024 record 2,694 human single units across six regions during an uncued serial reversal task and find the inference-linked reorganisation only in hippocampus: no other region changed shattering dimensionality, context or parity decodability, and **no other region held two variables in abstract format simultaneously** — amygdala (889 units), vmPFC (463), dACC (310) and preSMA (269) all recorded and all negative. VTC's context CCGP actually *decreased*. Under this reading the hippocampus is where the latent partition is constructed and prefrontal cortex consumes it | **Shared.** Bernardi et al. 2020 run the monkey original of the same task and find context, value and action in abstract format in **all three** recorded areas — hippocampus, DLPFC and ACC — with action abstract in DLPFC and ACC but *not* in hippocampus, and CCGP for context dropping on error trials in all three. Prefrontal cortex holds a variable hippocampus does not, which is not the consumer relationship | Whether a model needs one dedicated module that builds the abstract format, or whether the format is what every module in a control loop converges on when the same latent variable is worth carrying. Bears on [[wiki/concepts/complementary-learning-systems.md]]'s division of labour and on whether [[wiki/concepts/abstract-structural-codes.md]]'s `g` should be architecturally localised at all | `LIVE` — **and the two may not be in contradiction, because of an epoch confound the wiki should test before treating this as a disagreement.** Bernardi's prefrontal abstraction is measured in the 900 ms *before* stimulus onset; in the 900 ms *after*, DLPFC context CCGP falls to chance and ACC's is only weakly above it, while hippocampus keeps the format. If Courellis's analysis window is the post-stimulus one, B's prefrontal result and A's prefrontal null are the same fact at different times, and the real claim is that hippocampus is where the format is *maintained*, not where it is unique. Deciding it costs one re-analysis of an existing dataset ([[wiki/concepts/population-geometry.md]]) |
| T204 | **Does test-time compute buy generalization on ARC, or does it only buy the tail?** | **Compute is nearly irrelevant at the current frontier.** The two 2024 ARC Prize leaderboards differ by ~1,000× in budget per entry — Kaggle allows one P100 for 12 hours with no internet (~$10 of compute), ARC-AGI-Pub allows up to $10,000 in API credits — and their top scores landed 0.1 points apart (53.5% private vs. 53.6% semi-private). The report's own conclusion: *"algorithmic improvements towards AGI hold significant power and massive compute may not be necessary"*, reinforced by OmniARC reaching 40% with a 0.5B model inside the Kaggle budget ([[wiki/entities/arc-agi.md]]) | **Compute is the whole remaining gap, at a brutal exponent.** The same report estimates that Greenblatt's LLM-program-generation method would reach the 85% target at ~10⁸ generated-and-evaluated programs per task — a multi-million-dollar bill for 100 tasks — and states that a score can no longer be assigned to an approach, only to an approach *plus* a budget. The December 2024 o3-preview result is the same shape: 75% at low compute, ~87% at high, at ~$30,000/task ([[wiki/concepts/external-verification.md]]) | Decides which lever a builder pulls, and whether the wiki's benchmark rows need an inference-budget column or a compute-efficiency *score*. The two claims are formally compatible — the frontier is algorithm-limited while the tail is compute-limited — but only if the returns curve is flat then steep, which nobody has measured. If instead the 1,000× null reflects a *ceiling of the current approach class*, then both leaderboards are measuring the same missing mechanism and neither compute nor the next increment of engineering will move it | `LIVE`, but the curve now has its first segment. [[wiki/entities/poe-arc-solver.md]] sweeps one fixed method's inference budget over ~1 order of magnitude on ARC-AGI-1 public eval: DFS threshold `T` = 20% / 9% / 0.5% costs 14:12 / 20:50 / **134:43** hours and scores 70.0% / 71.6% / **71.8%** — 9.5× compute for **+1.8 points**, and the last 6.5× for +0.2, with candidate *coverage* still rising (73.5 → 76.0 → 83.5). So within one approach the returns are flat, and the reason is identified: the extra compute buys proposals a selector cannot use. That supports the left-hand column at the frontier and refines the right-hand one — the tail may be selector-limited rather than compute-limited. Still unrun: the same sweep over four orders of magnitude, and any sweep at all on ARC-AGI-2 |
| T205 | **Does ARC-AGI-1 measure abstraction across its whole task set, or only across a residue?** | **Roughly half of it does not.** An analysis of all 2020 competition submissions found **49% of the private evaluation set was solved by at least one entry, all of them brute-force DSL search** — before LLMs, at low compute. The report's own verdict is that this fraction "does not carry a useful signal towards AGI", i.e. those tasks have low developer-aware generalization difficulty and are reachable by enumeration over an authored vocabulary ([[wiki/entities/arc-agi.md]]) | **The benchmark's design claims every task requires recombining core-knowledge priors in a way that cannot be prepared for in advance**, and its resistance is evidence the claim holds: unbeaten for five years across a ~50,000× pretraining scale-up, with an $600,000 grand prize unclaimed and a ~30-point gap to the human 97–98% | Every ARC-AGI-1 score below ~50% reported anywhere in the wiki, including the wiki's own solver pages ([[wiki/entities/arc-vsa-solver.md]] 10.8% on Eval, [[wiki/entities/ilp-arc-synthesizer.md]] 5 hand-picked tasks): if the brute-forceable half is what a sub-50% method is covering, the score prices vocabulary coverage rather than abstraction, and two methods at equal score need not be equally far along. Also bears on the wiki's use of ARC as its G17 instrument — a benchmark can satisfy the whole checklist in [[wiki/concepts/skill-acquisition-efficiency.md]] and still be half-composed of `GD ≈ 0` items, because nothing in the checklist audits *per-task* difficulty | `LEANING` toward A, and the source concedes it: ARC-AGI-2 was built specifically to fix this, along with the small-private-set and inconsistent-difficulty problems. Resolvable directly — publish which 49 of the 100 private tasks fell to 2020 brute force and re-score every subsequent method on the complement |
| T206 | **What does a reported ARC-AGI-2 score denote?** | **Frontier models are at or past the Grand Prize threshold.** A third-party aggregator (`llm-stats.com`, July 2026, 16 models) reports GPT-5.5 at **0.850**, Gemini 3.1 Pro 0.771, GPT-5.4 0.733, Claude Opus 4.6 0.688 — i.e. the benchmark is effectively solved by closed frontier systems, with an average across all tracked models of 0.451 (`raw/llmstats-2026-arc-agi-2-leaderboard.md`) | **The verified open frontier is 24%, and the verified assisted frontier is 54%.** ARC Prize's own measurements: top Kaggle score 24.03% on the private set at $0.20/task after 1,455 teams and 15,154 entries; Gemini 3 Pro baseline **31%** at $0.81/task, rising to 54% only with an application-layer refinement harness at $31/task; scores below 5% not treated as signal at all (`raw/chollet-2025-arc-prize-report.md`, [[wiki/entities/arc-agi-2.md]]) | Every leaderboard row the wiki cites. The aggregator itself reports **0 verified and 13 self-reported** results, and does not state the eval subset (public / semi-private / private), the attempt budget (`pass@1` vs `pass@2`), the inference cost, or whether a harness was in the loop — and the report expects harnesses to be absorbed behind vendor APIs, which erases the model/loop boundary a vendor-reported number implicitly claims. So the two positions may not be measuring different systems so much as *different unstated protocols*, which is the failure mode [[wiki/concepts/external-verification.md]] catalogues for every math leaderboard | `LIVE`, and it is a reporting-protocol problem before it is an empirical one. Resolvable only from the ARC Prize side: any number not carrying (subset, attempts, $/task, harness yes/no) is uninterpretable and should not be entered in a wiki comparison table. Treat the aggregator row as `(tentative)` throughout |
| T207 | **Can a benchmark be simultaneously difficulty-calibrated and contamination-proof?** | **No — calibration is the leak.** ARC-AGI-2's goal 6 requires public, semi-private and private subsets to be drawn from demonstrably similar distributions (mean human accuracy matched to ≤1pp) so scores transfer across them. The 2025 report then states that a reasoning system trained on substantial public-domain data can "overfit" a private set precisely *because* it is i.i.d. with the public one, asserts this is now happening to ARC-AGI-1 and -2, and evidences it with Gemini 3 Deep Think emitting correct ARC colour names in a harness that never mentions ARC | **Yes — the private set still works, and the contamination is unquantified.** The same report cannot say whether the effect is accidental or deliberate, cannot measure its magnitude, and maintains that the format "provided a valuable scientific indicator". The empirical counter is the score structure itself: if distributional familiarity were doing the work, the 2024 ARChitects system would not have dropped from 56% to 2.5% on the re-authored set | Whether a private evaluation set is a durable instrument or a depreciating one, which decides how the wiki reads *every* held-out score, and whether G17's developer-blindness criterion needs a distributional clause on top of its item-level one. Note the two positions are compatible if contamination raises a floor without touching the ceiling — nobody has measured either | `LIVE`. The report's answer is not a fix but a policy: continual benchmark adaptation ([[wiki/concepts/refinement-loop.md]]), i.e. re-author faster than the field can absorb the distribution. The decisive experiment is cheap: score a frontier model on private eval tasks *stratified by similarity to the public training set* under any fixed embedding, and check whether score tracks similarity |
| T208 | **Does an application-layer harness measure a capability the model has, or one the harness author has?** | **The model's.** On ARC-AGI-2 a refinement harness takes a *frozen* Gemini 3 Pro from 31% to 54% at 38× cost, and Claude Opus 4.5 to ~54% with the same harness — the wiki's largest single measurement of what an acceptance test is worth, and its cleanest evidence that the rejector rather than the proposer is binding (G68). The harness is open-source and ARC-Prize-verified, and the ARC reports themselves expect good harness ideas to migrate behind vendor APIs, i.e. to be absorbed as model capability ([[wiki/entities/arc-agi-2.md]]) | **The author's, on the environments the author saw.** On ARC-AGI-3 the same class of intervention is bimodal: with harnesses built against three public environments and then tested on the full public set, Opus 4.6 goes 0.0% → **97.1%** on a variant of a seen environment class and 0.0% → **0.0%** on an unseen one, same model throughout. The foundation open-sourced a replay-driven harness scoring **100%** on the public set specifically to demonstrate that the number carries no information, and excludes harnesses from the official leaderboard entirely ([[wiki/entities/arc-agi-3.md]]) | Decides whether the wiki's rejector programme (G68) is a general architectural claim or a task-specific engineering one. Both measurements are real and they are not strictly contradictory — version 2's harness was evaluated on tasks its authors had not seen, version 3's transfer test is across *environments* rather than across items — but they license opposite bets. Under A, build the rejector and expect it to generalise; under B, a rejector is only as general as the failure mode it was written against, and the +23 points on version 2 should be re-measured on a set drawn after the harness was frozen. The discriminating experiment is cheap and neither report ran it: freeze one harness, evaluate on two sets differing only in whether the harness author saw their generating process | `LIVE` |
| T209 | **Should an efficiency metric charge for thinking?** | **No — actions are the resource.** ARC-AGI-3 counts only environment-changing actions; "internal operations that do not alter the environment, such as tool calls, reasoning steps, or retries within the model itself, are not counted". The stated justification is that action efficiency subsumes data, risk and time efficiency into one scalar that is directly comparable between a biological and an artificial agent, and that it penalises brute-force interaction, which is the failure mode the benchmark is built against. The environment is turn-based for the same reason: the intended difficulty is reasoning, not reflex ([[wiki/entities/arc-agi-3.md]]) | **Yes — a score attaches to (approach, compute budget) and never to an approach.** T204's claim, from ARC's own 2024 report, and the reason version 2 made cost-per-task a mandatory reporting column on every line. Under an action-only metric a system with unbounded per-action deliberation is maximally efficient by construction, and the exact strategy the metric is meant to penalise — search — simply moves inside the turn, where it is free. The benchmark's own cost axis (dollars per run, X-axis on the leaderboard) and its 5×-human-median action budget are administrative caps, not part of the score | Decides what "efficiency" names in the wiki's scoring vocabulary. The two metrics are measuring genuinely different resources — interaction with the world versus computation about it — and a human's 2.3-minute median on version 2 bounds *both* at once, which is why the human baseline reads as a single number and an AI's does not. **(brainstorm)** The repair is to report the pair rather than pick one: RHAE alongside compute-per-action, since a system matching human action counts at 10⁴× human deliberation has demonstrated planning and not efficiency, and the current metric cannot tell those apart | `LIVE` |
| T210 | **What does a super-human score on an abstraction benchmark denote?** | **Matrix reasoning is solved.** Zhang et al. 2018 measure humans at **84%** on RAVEN's test set, and several groups then report deep networks above that on RAVEN and on its repaired variants I-RAVEN and RAVEN-FAIR; on the original set the trained models here reach 73% (MRNet) and 89% (SCL) `(tentative — all reported second-hand through Odouard & Mitchell 2022)` | **The number is a purchase from the generator plus a leak from the answer set.** (i) RAVEN's distractors are the correct answer with one attribute perturbed, so a network trained *only on the eight candidates*, never shown the matrix, exceeds **90%**. (ii) The repairs fix the distractor generator and leave the item generator untouched. (iii) The same two models, on hand-authored items instantiating the *same* concepts they answer correctly i.i.d., fall to **49%/44%** and **62%/68%** — 21–29 points ([[wiki/entities/raven.md]]) | Every leaderboard-derived claim in the wiki, and specifically any comparison of a machine score against a human baseline collected on a *different* item distribution. The two positions are not formally contradictory — both scores are real — but they license opposite readings of the same model, and the discriminating evidence is one-sided: no human baseline was ever collected on the concept variations, so what is established is a large machine drop, not a human/machine gap. Repairs, both cheap and unrun: collect the human baseline on the variation sets, and report per-concept scores rather than a set mean **Root cause, named at the 212-ingest lint pass:** this is not a disagreement about the world but an unspecified reference protocol — see [[wiki/concepts/human-baseline.md]], parameters B1/B3/B5, and note that RAVEN's 84% fails B5 outright (it was never collected on the concept variations). | `LEANING` |
| T211 | **Should an intelligence benchmark equalise human priors, or withhold them?** | **Equalise.** A fair human/machine comparison requires the prior term `P` to be the same on both sides, and Core Knowledge is the only enumerated candidate for what humans start with — so a benchmark states its priors exhaustively, restricts every task to them, and thereby makes the comparison mean something ([[wiki/concepts/skill-acquisition-efficiency.md]], [[wiki/entities/arc-agi.md]]). Chollet's supporting argument is that intelligence must be tied to a defined area of application, and only the human one is accessible | **Withhold.** An artificial agent is not bound to the human domain or to the physical world, so it can be scored in arbitrary formalisable worlds — different dimensionality, altered physics or causality — and in such a world an installed human prior is a *mis-specified constraint*, not a head start. Objectness, action-on-contact and classical physics are named: classical physics is itself a pragmatic human model that contradicts quantum mechanics, so supplying it as an axiom would cap the agent at a known-false belief. Anchoring to humans also re-invites the skills-over-intelligence failure the field keeps repeating (Pfister & Jud 2025, [[wiki/concepts/problem-framing.md]]) | Decides whether [[wiki/concepts/core-knowledge.md]] is an ingredient of a reasoning architecture or only a scoring control — and whether the wiki's whole brain-inspired-prior programme is buying generality or buying a good fit to one world. It also decides what a benchmark may hold fixed: `P` can be equalised only by fixing it, and fixing it is what makes the framing constant (G73) | `LIVE` — the positions are not symmetric in what they can be tested. A benchmark over arbitrary worlds does not exist, and the source concedes its own residual (its worlds are still formalisable, executable and human-imagined), while the equalise side has five years of measurements. **(brainstorm)** The discriminating experiment is cheap and unrun: take an existing prior-equalised task set and *re-render it under a violated prior* (non-cohesive objects, non-Euclidean adjacency), then compare human and machine degradation. If installed priors are a head start, humans fall further than a prior-free learner; if they are a constraint, the ordering flips |
| T212 | **Does an LLM's demonstrated analogy competence extend to the ARC domain, or is it bound to the presentation format?** | **It extends — the competence is general.** Webb et al. 2022 report GPT-3/GPT-4 matching or exceeding human performance zero-shot on several idealized analogy domains, including Copycat-style letter-string analogies, which are *generative* (the answer is constructed, not selected) and are the closest published relative of ARC's task shape `(tentative — second-hand through Moskvichev et al. 2023; the primary is not in raw/)` | **It does not.** The same model, prompted in the same style, scores **0.03–0.33 per concept** on [[wiki/entities/conceptarc.md]] — 16 concepts authored to be *easy*, humans at 0.83–0.97 — with every output correctly formatted, so the floor is not a parsing artefact. The gap is not attributable to answer construction (letter-string analogies also construct) nor to few-shot budget (both give demonstrations) | Whether "LLMs do analogy" is a claim about a mechanism or about a modality. The discriminating variable the wiki can name: ARC grids are **2-D spatial structure serialised into a token sequence**, while letter strings are natively 1-D, so the ConceptARC floor may measure the cost of recovering a spatial parse from row-major text rather than any deficit in analogical mapping. **(brainstorm)** The experiment nobody in the wiki has run: the same tasks presented to the same model as an explicit object list (the parse supplied), which would separate the parse cost from the mapping cost — and which [[wiki/entities/arc-vsa-solver.md]]'s demonstration/query split (48.8 → 10.8) suggests is not the whole story | `LIVE`, with the wiki's own conjecture now **against** the evidence: Beger et al. 2025 run the same ConceptARC tasks through frontier models in both presentations and the *image* is worse by 40–60 accuracy points (o3 75.6% textual vs 29.2% visual with tools, 5.6% without), with the failure localised to perception rather than mapping ([[wiki/concepts/rule-level-evaluation.md]], T215). So row-major serialisation of 2-D structure is the **cheap** channel for these models, and the ConceptARC floor is not explained by it. The proposed experiment — supply the parse as an explicit object list — is still unrun, and is now the only live way to separate parse cost from mapping cost |
| T213 | **What is the human baseline on ARC-AGI-1?** | **~97–99%, and the gap to machines is ~30 points.** Two testers scored 97% and 98% on the private evaluation set and 100% between them; ten Mechanical Turk workers per task solved 99% of the public evaluation set between them. [[wiki/entities/arc-agi.md]] and [[wiki/empirical-tensions.md]] T205 both quote this range, and it is the number the "unsolved for five years" framing is measured against | **~60–84%, and the gap is much smaller.** Johnson et al. 2021 measure **83.8%** mean per-task accuracy over 95 participants on 40 public *training* tasks with 3 attempts (8.38 of 10 tasks per participant, hardest task 38.1%), and the ARC Prize's own reported *average* human on the evaluation set is **60.2%** — below several 2025 systems | Every "super-human" and "below human" claim on this benchmark, including the ARC Prize's framing of [[wiki/entities/poe-arc-solver.md]]'s 71.6% as above human. The three numbers are not in conflict about the world; they measure different things — **union over testers** (solvability of the task), **per-subject rate on the easy split** (difficulty for an individual), and **mean over a crowd on the hard split** (population competence) — on three different task pools. What is wrong is treating any of them as *the* baseline. It also bears on Chollet's own G31 workaround, which proposes human success rate as the empirical proxy for generalization difficulty without specifying which of these it is **Root cause:** aggregation order (B3) left unstated — union-over-testers, per-subject mean and crowd mean are three different estimands, and the fix is the five-parameter specification at [[wiki/concepts/human-baseline.md]]. | `RESOLVED` as a category error, `LIVE` as a reporting defect: no source in the wiki reports per-subject first-attempt accuracy on the *evaluation* split, which is the only quantity commensurable with a machine's `pass@2` score on the same tasks. Costs one crowd study to fix |
| T214 | **What is the human baseline on ConceptARC?** | **~91% mean per-concept.** 415 participants, 8–14 per test input, three attempts each, averaged over participants per test input and then over the 16 concept groups — 0.83–0.97 across concepts, and the number the benchmark's founding 40-point human−machine gap is measured against ([[wiki/entities/conceptarc.md]]) | **73% `pass@1`.** Beger et al. 2025 re-analyse the *same* study's unpublished per-response data on the same 480 items and report first-attempt accuracy, which is below o3's 75.6–77.1% textual accuracy and below o4-mini's 77.7% | Every "models now match/exceed humans on ConceptARC" claim, and the direction of the gap this benchmark exists to report. The two numbers are not in conflict about the world — three attempts versus one, per-concept mean versus per-item pooled — but they license opposite headlines, and only the second is commensurable with a machine `pass@1`. It is T213's defect recurring on the sister benchmark, and here the fix has already been paid for: the raw data supports both, so any paper using either should state which **Root cause:** attempt budget (B2) left unstated, the parameter that most often breaks commensurability because machine scores are almost always `pass@1` — [[wiki/concepts/human-baseline.md]]. | `RESOLVED` as a protocol difference, `LIVE` as a reporting defect — the wiki should quote 73% against machine `pass@1` and ~91% only against a 3-attempt machine score, and no source reports the latter |
| T215 | **Is the visual/textual gap on ARC-format tasks a reasoning deficit or a perceptual one?** | **Reasoning.** Rule correctness itself falls with the modality, not just accuracy: o3 states the intended rule on 57.3% of tasks in text and 40.0% in vision, Claude on 57.5% and 14.4%, Gemini on 46.1% and 23.6% — and intended-rule *coverage* over the 480 tasks drops 85.8→58.5 (o3), 72.1→17.3 (Claude), 67.9→29.3 (Gemini). Something above perception degrades | **Perception.** Rule–grid alignment stays 93.6–97.4% in vision, so the models still execute what they state; the separately-scored *visual error* rate (grid dimensions misread, objects misplaced, colour↔integer mis-mapped) is **49.1% / 59.7% / 77.3%**; giving the model Python — i.e. computer-vision libraries to parse the image — is worth +12 to +24 points in vision and ~0 to −5 in text, while extra reasoning effort is worth +9 to +19 in text and ~0 in vision. The Bongard-problem and RAVEN literatures report the same attribution `(tentative — second-hand)` | What to build. Under A the visual pathway needs a better reasoner; under B it needs a parser, and the reasoning stack is already adequate. It also decides how to read every ARC score in the wiki, since all of them are obtained on the *textual* serialisation, which this result makes the **easier** modality — retiring the wiki's standing conjecture that row-major serialisation of 2-D structure is what floors LLMs on ARC (T212) | `LEANING B` — B has the direct measurements and A's evidence is not independent of them, since a rule can only be judged intended if the grid was parsed well enough to describe. The discriminating experiment is cheap and unrun: supply the *parse* (an object list) with the image and re-score |
| T216 | **Does a human adversary paid to fool one model find that model's idiosyncratic blind spots, or the model class's?** | **The model class's.** In rounds 2 and 3 of ANLI the adversary was an *ensemble* of differently-seeded models, and the base model still scores **19.3 and 20.4 against a 33.3 chance level** on the round it authored — below chance, not at it, so the annotators found a systematic wrong belief shared across seeds rather than one parameter vector's noise. The exclusive-annotator test subset tracks the main test set within ~4 points, which also rules out the items being artefacts of particular writers' style. And the discovered weakness class transfers to a differently-authored probe: adding the data lifts an independent numerical-reasoning stress test by **+18.5** points (Nie et al. 2020, [[wiki/entities/anli.md]]) | **Partly that one model's.** In the same results table, BERT and XLNet — trained on identical data, including all three ANLI rounds — **beat RoBERTa on A2 and A3, the two rounds RoBERTa itself seeded** (50.7 / 48.3 vs 48.9 / 44.4). If the vulnerabilities were purely class-general, an architecture that never served as the adversary would not find them easier. The authors' own reading is that the three models have different weaknesses, plausibly inherited from their different pretraining corpora | Whether an adversarially-collected benchmark is a *benchmark* or a *report on one system*. If A, the loop is the wiki's cheapest o.o.d. instrument and its scores are comparable across systems; if B, each round measures its own adversary and cross-system comparison is invalid — which also decides whether the +27.5-point matched-budget data-efficiency result (G32) is a general curriculum finding or a fact about RoBERTa. Nothing in the paper separates the two: no round was collected against two architectures in parallel, which is the one-line experiment that would | `LIVE` |
| T217 | **Does conditioning on a self-generated intermediate trace help or hurt?** | **Helps — it is the mechanism, and it was measured in the same year as B.** Cobbe et al. 2021: finetuning GPT-3 6B to emit the final answer *without* intermediate steps drops GSM8K from **20.6% to 5.2%**, a 4× loss from deleting self-generated scratch space at a scale and date comparable to B ([[wiki/entities/gsm8k.md]]). [[wiki/concepts/refinement-loop.md]] names chain-of-thought/latent-space refinement as one of six substrates behind every 2025 top ARC-AGI-2 score, and [[wiki/concepts/external-verification.md]] records the era's headline numbers as a *search over traces* (o1 at 74% greedy → 93% verifier-selected on AIME 2024). Higher reasoning modes buy points; Gemini 3 Deep Think spends 138,000 reasoning tokens on a task the Pro model answers in 96 | **Hurts, measured on the same model.** Hendrycks et al. 2021: prompting GPT-2 1.5B for a full step-by-step solution before the boxed answer drops MATH accuracy **6.9% → 5.3%**, while *training* on the identical solutions raises it (6.3 → 6.9) and *reading* 99% of a ground-truth one raises it to ~40%. So the trace is valuable as external supervision and as external context, and negative as self-produced context. The authors' mechanism is snowballing: a mistake in the prefix derails the continuation ([[wiki/entities/math-dataset.md]]) | Whether "let it think longer" is an architectural principle or a regime. Under the threshold reading, the intervention has a per-step-reliability precondition `p*` and a system below it is made worse by its own tokens — which predicts that reasoning modes cannot rescue a weak base model, and that the 2021 result reproduces today under temperature or model-size degradation. It also decides whether the rejector is optional: every post-2021 success either raises `p` or escapes the single-trajectory regime by adding a checker | `LEANING A`, and the row is now a **threshold rather than a disagreement**. Both sides are controlled within-model ablations from 2021 at comparable scale, and they differ in sign: GSM8K (2–8 elementary steps, arithmetic offloaded to a calculator) −4× without the trace, MATH (competition) −1.6 points *with* it. The free variable that reconciles them is per-step reliability `p`, and GSM8K raises `p` by an explicit intervention, which makes the pair the closest thing to a two-point measurement of `p*`. The decisive experiment is still unrun and is now cheap to state: re-run Cobbe's ablation with the calculator annotations disabled and find the crossing |
| T218 | **Is normalised exact match an adequate acceptance test?** | **Yes, near-completely.** Hendrycks et al. 2021 designed the `\boxed{}` delimiter plus a normalisation ruleset (fraction encodings, units, spacing, factor and variable order, decimal/fraction equivalence) and state these "cover nearly all ways that different generated or actual solutions can be equivalent in practice" — which is what makes free-generation mathematics auto-gradeable at all, and what every RLVR pipeline and every MATH-derived reward model is built on ([[wiki/entities/math-dataset.md]]) | **No — the false-negative rate is large enough to corrupt training.** Raiyan et al. 2026 report that up to **38%** of responses flagged incorrect by rule-based graders were correct (`12/36` vs `1/3`), which denies the model informative gradient and reinforces whatever surface format the grader happens to accept ([[wiki/concepts/external-verification.md]]) | Every outcome-supervised training run and every reported MATH-family score. A 38% false-negative rate on the reward channel is not a measurement nuisance — under RLVR it is an optimisation pressure toward *grader-legible* answers, which is the Goodhart row of that page's failure table entered through the cheapest rung rather than through a learned verifier | `LEANING B` — B is a direct audit of graders in use, A is a design claim the authors never measured. Neither source reports the rate on MATH specifically with its own normaliser, which is the missing measurement |
| T219 | **What is a scaling extrapolation evidence for?** | **For the task's hardness.** Hendrycks et al. 2021 fit a log-linear trend across GPT-2 0.1B→1.5B, project ~10³⁵ parameters for 40% on MATH, and conclude that scale will not deliver mathematical problem solving — the same argument form used for [[wiki/entities/arc-agi.md]] and [[wiki/entities/arc-agi-2.md]], where flat or near-zero scores across four orders of magnitude of pretraining are read as evidence about the *ability* | **For one algorithm family, and nothing else.** MATH was above 90% within roughly three years, via chain-of-thought, verifier-selected sampling and RL on verifiable rewards — none of which is a parameter count. The curve was measured on answer-only fine-tuned decoders and was silent about a change of inference procedure; the paper's own *conclusion* ("we will need new algorithmic advancements") was correct precisely because its curve was not a forecast. The irony is exact: the intervention that broke the extrapolation is the one the same paper measured as **negative** (T217) | Every "scale will not solve X" claim in the wiki, and the interpretation of a flat benchmark curve generally. Under B, such a curve is a statement that the *current* method is not converging and carries no information about the discontinuity that a method change can produce — so it can never license an architectural conclusion, only a "not this way" | `LEANING B` — B is the one case in the wiki where the question is settled by outcome, and it settles it against A. The residual for A: MATH's saturation still required a new algorithm, i.e. the paper's qualitative claim survived its quantitative one, so the honest export is "a flat curve licenses *not this method*, never *not this ability*" |
| T220 | **Is best-of-`k` monotone in `k`?** | **Yes — more search is more score.** The wiki's headline inference-scaling numbers are all monotone and taken at large budgets: o1 at 93% with 1,000 candidates plus a learned scorer, DeepSeek-Prover-V2 at 88.9% with 8,192 against a Lean kernel, [[wiki/entities/neo-neural-theorizer.md]] at ~0.70 with 1,024 and a majority vote, [[wiki/entities/poe-arc-solver.md]] still gaining coverage at 6.5× runtime ([[wiki/concepts/external-verification.md]]) | **No — it is an inverted U, measured.** Cobbe et al. 2021 rank up to several thousand completions per GSM8K problem with a learned 6B verifier: accuracy rises to about **400 candidates and then falls**, the authors' stated cause being that a large enough search *finds adversarial solutions that fool the verifier* ([[wiki/entities/gsm8k.md]]) | Decides whether inference budget is a dial a builder can simply turn up, and whether a reported best-of-`k` score needs a *peak* reported alongside it. It also sets which rung of [[wiki/concepts/external-verification.md]]'s ladder can absorb a large budget at all: if the turn-down is a property of learned scorers, then sound checkers (kernel, execution, invariance veto) and learned ones have qualitatively different scaling laws, and Goodhart is not a risk that grows with `k` but a *regime* that begins at a measurable `k` | `LEANING` toward a reconciliation neither source states: the sign of `d(score)/dk` is set by whether the acceptance test can be satisfied by a candidate that is wrong. **A third measurement now constrains that reconciliation and does not fit it:** [[wiki/entities/hle.md]] bins output length on a `log2` scale and finds accuracy log-linear in reasoning tokens and then *falling* beyond **2^14 ≈ 16,384** — with no candidate set, no scorer and no selection, so nothing can be fooled. The budget there is spent inside one trajectory, which makes the turn-down a per-step-reliability ceiling (T217's `p`, compounding as ~`(1−ε)^L`) rather than a Goodhart threshold. Two inverted-Us, two causes: the verifier peak should move with a sounder checker, the length peak only with a lower `ε`. Both monotone rows above use a sound or generator-independent test; the turn-down row uses a learned scalar. Unrun: the same sweep on a modern learned reward model, which would say whether the peak moved or vanished — and no wiki source reports a best-of-`k` curve past its own maximum |
| T221 | **Is expert domain knowledge "crystallized" in the sense that excludes it from an intelligence measure?** | **Yes — accumulated content, not mechanism.** The ARC framing excludes crystallized ability from the target because it is culturally acquired and not comparable across test-takers, and states the design rule *easy for humans, hard for AI*: a task both humans and machines find hard "isolates only accumulated knowledge", whereas a task humans solve effortlessly and machines cannot isolates a missing mechanism ([[wiki/concepts/skill-acquisition-efficiency.md]]). On that reading a graduate-science benchmark measures the size of a retrievable store, and a retriever should substitute for it | **No — what the expert has is not retrievable.** Rein et al. 2023 run the control: PhDs in *other* sciences, paid a $30-per-item bonus to succeed, with unrestricted internet and no time limit (median 30 min, mean 37, top quintile ≥45), reach **34%** against in-domain experts' **65%** and chance 25%. The machine side agrees — a search tool moves GPT-4 by +0.7 points. And the gap runs **opposite** to machine difficulty (chemistry Δ=40.6 is GPT-4's worst domain at 31.8%; biology Δ=23.5 is its best at 58.1%), so "hard for both" is not one quadrant. The six printed items are multi-hop chains whose every hop is individually look-up-able, which locates the gap in *selection and composition* rather than in storage | Decides whether knowledge-loaded benchmarks are admissible evidence about a reasoning architecture at all, and therefore whether a brain-inspired reasoner should be scored on any of them. If the gap is compositional, GPQA and ARC are measuring overlapping mechanisms under opposite selection rules and the exclusion argument loses its basis; if it is storage, the 34% needs another explanation | `LIVE` — no experiment separates the two. The decisive one is cheap and unbuilt: perturb GPQA items at the hop level using the reasoning chains already shipped with each question (the GSM-Symbolic protocol, [[wiki/entities/gsm8k.md]]). If scores fall under matched re-instantiation, the competence is compositional and the exclusion argument fails; if they hold, it is a store. Note both sides are also bounded by the same instrument's noise floor — GPQA's own objectivity is 73.6–76.4% ([[wiki/entities/gpqa.md]]) |
| T222 | **Does keeping the evaluation set permanently private solve contamination?** | **Yes — it is the only defence that cannot decay.** Glazer et al. 2024 ([[wiki/entities/frontiermath.md]]) commission every item new from research mathematicians under an originality requirement, route submissions through encrypted channels and password-protected archives, scan the whole set with two plagiarism services (no significant matches), release **five** problems and keep the rest unpublished with the authoring organisation running all evaluations. There is no public split to be i.i.d. with, so the distributional-leakage channel [[wiki/entities/arc-agi-2.md]] documents (T207) has no route in, and no item can be memorised because no item is available to memorise. | **No — it relocates the failure from leakage into unauditability.** `F1` in [[wiki/concepts/certification-instruments.md]]: developer-blindness is a depleting stock, and a fixed private set absorbs leakage through repeated scoring regardless of publication (ARC-AGI-1's 100 private tasks absorbed ~10,000 reported scores). The specific cost here is measurable: the same paper estimates its own **critical error rate at ~10%** from a 35-item second-review sample against a 0–2% spread between the six models it ranks, reports **6/35** items with missing hypotheses and **2/35** violating its own guessproofness bound — and nobody outside the organisation can check any of it, reproduce a score, or re-audit an item. A measured error rate is a property of the audit, and this audit is not repeatable by a third party. | Every score reported on a closed benchmark, and the design choice facing any successor to a saturated public one: the two properties (contamination resistance, external auditability) are traded directly against each other, and the wiki's other self-auditing benchmark ([[wiki/entities/gpqa.md]], 73.6–76.4% objectivity) sits on the opposite side of the trade with a canary string as its only leakage defence. | `LIVE` — and a **third position** now exists that neither A nor B anticipated: [[wiki/entities/olymmath.md]] sources its 350 items from printed, non-digitised material, publishes all of them, and then **measures** the residual leakage (`n`-gram `δ` against LLM-rewritten variants: 0.90% / 17.59% for two base models in English, against 34.84% / 38.81% for PolyMath). That buys auditability and contamination resistance at once, at the price of a defence that decays from the day of release — to which the paper's answer is to open-source the *construction pipeline* (the Lean formalisation agent, the sourcing protocol) so the set can be refreshed, i.e. **a benchmark as a renewable process rather than an artefact**. The unsolved part is comparability: successive editions share no anchor subset, so a score trend across refreshes means nothing. [[wiki/entities/hle.md]] takes the same third position (all 2,500 items public, a private set held back, and HLE-Rolling as the refresh mechanism) and adds the measurement that most weakens **A**: publishing the set is what makes a **15.4%** error rate knowable at all, and the number turns out to depend on the reviewing rule (18% → 25% on the same items), so FrontierMath's unauditable ~10% is not comparable to it in either direction — `F12`. |
| T223 | **Does a cross-lingual score gap measure the model's reasoning, or the harness's answer extraction?** | **Reasoning.** The gap is a property of the model: every one of 14 models (1.5B / 7B / 14B) scores higher on the English than the Chinese version of the *identical* problem, the difference is significant by Wilcoxon signed-rank across all four subjects and both difficulty levels over 582k released trajectories, and the authors' explanation is a pretraining-distribution one — English corpora dominate, so English-language problem solving is generally stronger (Sun et al. 2025, [[wiki/entities/olymmath.md]]). Read this way, language is the strongest content-preserving perturbation available (every token changes, no relation does) and the drop is `I14`'s rephrasing axis taken to its limit. | **The grader.** The same paper's own trajectory analysis reports that **answer-extraction failures are disproportionately frequent among incorrect Chinese responses** — a presentation-layer defect that the authors themselves call "distinct from reasoning errors". Normalised exact match is assumed language-neutral everywhere in the wiki and is not: what the grader can *find* in a response depends on the language it was written in, so every cross-lingual comparison is a joint measurement of the model and the harness. The confound cannot be re-attributed to leakage either — the paper's `δ` metric is higher in English for OlymMATH, whose English version is an author-produced translation that could not have been in any pretraining corpus, so `δ` is measuring English fluency as much as exposure and is not comparable across languages. | Whether a brain-inspired reasoner's competence is language-bound, and more immediately whether the wiki may read *any* multilingual score difference as evidence about representation. It also bounds `I14`: a "meaning-preserving" perturbation must preserve the **grader's** competence as well as the problem's semantics, which no perturbation set in the wiki checks. | `LIVE`. The decisive experiment is cheap and the data already exists: re-grade the released Chinese trajectories with a language-matched extractor (or hand-score a stratified sample of the extraction failures) and re-run the Wilcoxon. If the gap survives at the same magnitude it is the model; if it collapses it was the harness. Nobody has done it, and the trajectories are public. |
| T224 | **Does a correct external worked solution to a closely related problem help or hurt?** | **Helps, strongly, and it is the wiki's cleanest positive.** Hendrycks et al. 2021 ([[wiki/entities/math-dataset.md]]) hand a model an increasing fraction of the ground-truth derivation and read accuracy off the hint curve: 0% of the solution ≈ 6%, **99% of the solution ≈ 40%**, a +34-point gain from conditioning on a correct trace produced outside the model. The same asymmetry organises the wiki's three-way trace decomposition — a correct *external* trace is positive as training signal and very positive as conditioning input, and only the *self-generated, unverified* trace is negative. Every retrieval-augmented and few-shot mathematics pipeline is built on this reading. | **Hurts as often as it helps, once the trace is of a *different* problem.** Huang et al. 2025 ([[wiki/entities/math-perturb.md]]) supply the original problem **and its correct solution** as a one-shot example for a perturbation at embedding-cosine ~0.99 whose method no longer applies. The effect splits: 24–40% of prior errors are repaired for large closed models, and **18–40% of previously correct items are broken**, netting under 5%. The misleading half *grows with model capability* — 18–40% for large models against 4–15% for small open ones — so competence at in-context learning and vulnerability to an inapplicable demonstration are the same competence. | The reconciling variable is whether the supplied trace is a trace **of the problem being asked**, and the wiki had been treating *external and correct* as sufficient. It bites wherever demonstrations are selected by embedding similarity — retrieval-augmented generation, few-shot prompt construction, and [[wiki/concepts/test-time-training.md]]'s fine-tune-on-the-neighbourhood move, against which the authors state the hypothesis explicitly. The missing measurement is a hint curve run on a *perturbed* item, i.e. accuracy as a function of the fraction of a derivation that is correct **for a problem one edit away**; nobody has drawn it. | `LIVE` |
| T225 | **Can a benchmark whose label-noise floor exceeds every score reported on it order the systems it ranks?** | **Yes — the noise is unbiased and the ordering survives it.** Phan et al. 2025/2026 report 2,500 items and an 8-model leaderboard spanning 2.7–13.4% ([[wiki/entities/hle.md]]) alongside a self-measured **15.4%** expert disagreement rate. If mislabelled items are equally hard for every model, the error rate is a constant offset that cancels in a *difference* of scores, and the binomial standard error at `n = 2,500` is ~0.7pp, so a 10-point spread is many standard errors wide. This is the implicit position of every leaderboard built on the dataset, and of the wiki's other self-auditing benchmarks ([[wiki/entities/gpqa.md]] at 73.6–76.4% objectivity, [[wiki/entities/frontiermath.md]] at ~10% critical errors), none of which withdraws a ranking after measuring its own floor. | **No — the offset is not constant, and the floor is not even a fixed number.** The disagreement rate is a function of the audit protocol, not of the items: the *same* biology/chemistry/health subset yields **18%** under multi-reviewer-with-rebuttal and **25%** under a single dissenting expert. A rate that moves 7 points on the reviewing rule cannot be subtracted. Worse, the errors are unlikely to be model-independent — an ambiguous item is one where several answers are defensible, which is precisely where a model's chance of matching the recorded key depends on its output style and on the LLM judge's matching behaviour (T218). And the authors themselves disclaim the ordering on the *other* ground (`F7`): inflections near zero "are not strongly indicative of progress". | Every score in the 2.7–13.4% band, and by extension every frontier-benchmark leaderboard placed deliberately on the floor. It is distinct from `F11` ([[wiki/concepts/certification-instruments.md]]): F11 is insufficient `n`, which shrinks with more items; this is a bias term of unknown sign that does not. It also decides whether "measure your own error rate" is a virtue or a formality — three benchmarks in the wiki now report one, and no reported score anywhere has been adjusted by it. | `LEANING`, and the answer is split. **The decisive measurement has now been run** — Zhai et al. 2026 ([[wiki/entities/hle-verified.md]]) repaired 1,143 items and re-scored seven frontier models on both the full set and the repaired subset. **A wins on the ordering**: all seven hold rank after repair, so a floor exceeding every score does not by itself invalidate a ranking. **B wins on the offset**: the gains span +7.78 to +11.50, so the noise is not the constant that cancels in a difference, and adjacent gaps halve (Claude-Opus4.5 over DeepSeek-V3.2: 5.10 → 2.40). And on the defective quarter alone, B wins outright — the same models compress from a **2.5× to a 1.22× spread** and the top rank changes hands, so a sub-leaderboard restricted to the hardest items was almost entirely an artefact. The same audit puts the floor itself at ~26% scoring-relevant rather than 15.4% (T226). Corrected statement: *the ordering survives, the gaps do not, and the harder the subset the less of it is real.* Still open for benchmarks that have not been repaired, which is all of the others. |
| T226 | **What is a benchmark's error rate, when two audits of the same 2,500 items disagree by a factor of 1.7 — and can a model-in-the-loop audit measure its own effect on model scores?** | **15.4%, by the authors' own multi-reviewer-with-rebuttal protocol.** Phan et al. 2025/2026 ([[wiki/entities/hle.md]]) sample 400 items across two rounds, have students solve them in full, and route every flagged error between organisers, original authors and auditors until consensus — an item counts as bad only if the author cannot defend it. The rebuttal round exists precisely to stop a single dissenting reviewer from manufacturing an error, and the same paper shows dropping it inflates the rate on a subset from 18% to 25%. On this reading the higher external number is a *protocol artefact* of an auditor with no author in the loop. | **~26% scoring-relevant, 45.7% repairable, 27.6% indeterminate.** Zhai et al. 2026 ([[wiki/entities/hle-verified.md]]) audit **all** 2,500 items component-wise and three-valued, and find only 668 usable unmodified. The scoring-relevant figure is not asserted but derived, and it is unusually robust: the ratio Δ(full set)/Δ(repaired subset) is 0.24–0.29 for each of seven models computed independently, which is hard to produce from an over-eager auditor. Its own weakness is the mirror image of A's: the flagging stage is pass@8 frontier solvers and the repair stage is model-proposed and model-adjudicated, while the reported success metric is **frontier-model accuracy** — so items where the models are confidently and uniformly wrong are exactly the ones at risk of being repaired into agreement, and the post-repair clustering of seven models into a nine-point band is the signature that would show it. | Every number in the wiki of the form "benchmark X self-reports an error rate of Y". If the rate is a function of the audit's reviewer count, rebuttal rule, unit of judgement (item vs component) and label arity (binary vs three-valued), then `F12` is not a caveat to attach to a benchmark but a statement that **the quantity is undefined until the protocol is specified**, exactly as [[wiki/concepts/human-baseline.md]] concluded for the human denominator (T210/T213/T214 read as one defect). It also decides whether I18-style repair is a fix or a contamination channel: a benchmark repaired by the systems it scores has no remaining developer-blindness on the repaired items. | `LIVE`. Two cheap controls, neither run, and either settles it: (i) report the fraction of revised items whose new answer equals the plurality pass@8 model answer, against the base rate on gold items the models also failed — the pass@8 outputs are already collected; (ii) repair a random sample **blind**, with a third expert team given no model outputs, and report key agreement with the model-assisted repair. |
| T227 | **Does a near-zero train−test familiarity disparity license the verdict "this model did not train on the benchmark"?** | **Yes, as reported.** Xu et al. 2024's headline instrument is `δ_train-test`, models are *ordered* by it across 31 systems, and the paper's own reading is that "models such as the LLaMA series and Mistral-7B-v0.1 exhibit nearly zero `δ_train-test` scores, suggesting minimal potential for benchmark leakage". Every downstream citation of a leakage ranking inherits that ordering ([[wiki/concepts/benchmark-contamination.md]]) | **No — the statistic's null and its worst case coincide.** A model that ingested *both* splits reads the same near-zero disparity as one that ingested neither, and the paper supplies the counterexample in its own table: ChatGLM3-6B at `δ_train-test = 0.55` sits among the clean models on `δ_train = 36.85` / `δ_test = 36.30`, ~3× LLaMA-7B's absolute familiarity. The instance-level backstop fails too, and on a *known positive*: Aquila2-34B, documented as having seen the entire GSM8K test set, returns **zero** suspicious items on it because it learned the content under a different answer delimiter | Every leakage ranking, and every wiki claim of the form "benchmark X is uncontaminated for model Y". It also bounds [[wiki/entities/olymmath.md]]'s self-audit, which reports `δ` on a single split with no disparity available, and it is the reason `F14` was added to [[wiki/concepts/certification-instruments.md]] rather than being folded into `F1` | `LEANING` to B — B is the authors' own stated limitation, and the ChatGLM3 and Aquila2-34B rows are in their own tables. The repair is arithmetic and unrun: report the triple `(δ_train, δ_test, δ_train-test)` and treat *high-and-flat* as the both-leaked signature, then calibrate the absolute scale against a model with a documented corpus |
| T228 | **Does model scale reduce or amplify reliance on shortcuts?** | **Amplifies.** On Shortcut Suite ([[wiki/entities/shortcut-suite.md]]) LLaMA2-Chat under few-shot ICL falls monotonically with parameter count on exactly the non-entailment items the heuristic gets wrong — Lexical Overlap `¬E` 75.3 → 48.5 → **3.6** and Subsequence `¬E` 59.5 → 12.4 → **3.1** for 7B → 13B → 70B — while the same models' Standard accuracy *rises* (42.1 → 59.1 → 57.8). The 70B model is a near-perfect constant `entailment` classifier. It is not a one-family artefact: LLaMA3 zero-shot Constituent `¬E` goes 8B **40.1** → 70B **11.1** | **Reduces, given the right prompt.** Under chain-of-thought the ordering reverses and LLaMA2-Chat-70B leads its family on most sets (Constituent `¬E` 25.3 against 41.7/34.1 — mixed, but Standard 60.3/70.9 is the family best and the CoT gains are largest at 13B–70B: +40.9 for 13B on Constituent `¬E`). The authors' reading: larger models retain better semantic comprehension and need the prompt to elicit it rather than lacking the competence | Decides whether shortcut resistance is a capability that scaling buys. If A is the whole story, every i.i.d. scaling curve in the wiki is uninformative about robustness and the fix must go on one of the four levers. If B is, the competence is present and the defect is in *elicitation* — which relocates the problem to [[wiki/concepts/cognitive-control.md]] and makes it a deployment question. The two are not strictly contradictory (they differ in prompt) and that is the finding: **the sign of the scaling effect on robustness is a property of the prompt, so no scaling claim about shortcut reliance is well-posed without one.** The discriminating experiment is unrun and cheap — hold the prompt fixed and sweep scale on the `¬E` arm alone across ≥3 families |
| T229 | **Does making the latent structure legible and editable make a rewriting environment tractable?** | **Yes — legibility, sparsity, boundedness and meta-stationarity are the conditions, and they are what the lift needs.** [[wiki/concepts/latent-graph-discovery.md]]'s treatment of hardness source 6 argues that lifting rule-state into the node, `s' = (base_state, rule_config)`, restores stationarity, and that the only obstacle is tractability of the lifted space `(base) × (rule-configs)` — learnable whenever rule-config factorises and rewrites are sparse, legible, bounded and meta-stationary (gap G7). On that reading a domain meeting all five clauses should be the easy case, and G8's reification requirement is discharged for free when the rule is already an object | **No — a domain meeting every clause is solved by nobody.** [[wiki/entities/baba-is-ai.md]] renders each rule as three movable word tiles, active iff horizontally aligned: ~15 possible rules, one push per rewrite, the rule written in words inside the observation, and an activation predicate that never changes in any level. GPT-4o reaches **100%** on the same environments when obeying the rules suffices, and GPT-4o / Gemini-1.5-Pro / Gemini-1.5-Flash reach **17.33 / 14.67 / 20.0%** when winning requires composing two edits — failing the held-out `break; make; goto` under every rotation of which plan shape is withheld, with all three primitives demonstrated in context. Adding a *causally inactive* rule lowers accuracy at every rung of the distractor ladder, so even reading the rule state is not free. **And the reference system is not a counterexample**: [[wiki/entities/fluxx.md]] satisfies the same five clauses with a rule-config of a few named slots, face up in English, one rewrite per play and a countable generator, and experienced human players report losing the combined effect at ten to eleven concurrent rules — one of which created a live win condition nobody noticed for several turns `(tentative)`. `|rule_config|` is multiplicative in the live slots whether or not each slot is legible | Whether G7 is a statement about learnability or only about well-posedness, and where G8's residue lives. The conditions make the problem well-posed; nothing in the wiki searches over rewrites, and the models that parse the rules perfectly never consider editing them — which relocates the gap to the selector (G61) and to subgoal decomposition (G33) rather than to the representation | `LIVE` — one benchmark, three models of one 2024 vintage, no RL agent trained in the environment, no human baseline, and SD > mean on the hard set; the human-side datum is one anecdote in a product review and carries no protocol |
| T230 | **When the rewrite machinery is itself rewritable, is there anything left to learn?** | **No — and the wiki already says so.** [[wiki/concepts/latent-graph-discovery.md]]'s treatment of hardness source 6 states that a rewrite process with *no compressible generator* is unsolvable in principle, for any learner, because there is nothing to learn. [[wiki/entities/nomic.md]] is the limit case: proposals are free natural-language text (unbounded vocabulary), the amendment and precedence rules are themselves amendable (no meta-stationarity), and the scoring rule and winning threshold are ordinary mutable rules, so even the reward is inside the rewritten object. There is no stationary kernel over `rule_config` to infer, and the two-tier W/M assignment — generator to slow **W**, active edge set to fast **M** — has nothing to put in W. | **Yes, and the generator is simply not in the rules.** The game has been played since 1982 and games terminate with winners, so *something* makes the rewrite stream predictable enough to act on. Suber locates it outside the rule text: game rules derive authority from the players' agreement rather than by inference from prior rules, so Hart's **acceptance** model applies where Ross's inference model does not — the compressible structure is the population of proposers and the ratification threshold they will clear, not the rule system. The rule set then *bounds* what that population can do to the episode: at most 25 concurrent mutable rules, one rule-change per turn, no retroactive effect, an initial set deliberately built so that no single rule-change can destroy continuity, and the objective's *form* entrenched against amendment. On this reading the learnable object is a model of the other agents plus a set of hard bounds, which is ordinary [[wiki/concepts/contextual-inference.md]] over a non-stationary but *rate-limited* latent. | Decides whether hardness source 6 has a solvable interior or only a solvable boundary. If A holds, G9 is not an architecture problem — a self-amending system is unlearnable from the inside and the only question is what to entrench, which makes the interesting work the *design* of the immutable tier. If B holds, the generator was never supposed to be in the environment: it is in the other agents, and G9 collapses into multi-agent modelling under rate limits, which relocates it beside [[wiki/entities/autotom.md]] rather than beside [[wiki/concepts/meta-optimized-plasticity.md]]. Nothing decides it today, because the domain has never been implemented and no system, human or machine, has been measured on it — the wiki's only self-amending domain is also its only domain with **zero** data | `LIVE`, and it is a tension about the *object of learning* rather than about a result. The cheap discriminator is B's own claim: run the same self-amending environment with the proposal distribution held fixed and known, and see whether performance is recovered — if it is, the generator was in the agents |
| T231 | **Is the accuracy of deep spiking networks evidence about spiking *learning*, or only about spiking *inference*?** | **Learning — the gap to conventional deep nets is closing.** The standard summary of the field, and the one this wiki's SNN page carried until now: spiking networks lag but the gap is decreasing, vanishes on some tasks, and is bought with far fewer operations (Tavanaei et al. 2019, abstract) | **Inference only.** The same review's comparison table splits into two tracks, and they are anti-correlated. Offline: train a rate network by backpropagation, copy the weights, replace activations with firing rates — highest accuracy, the only track reported past MNIST (CIFAR-10, ImageNet), and **nothing was learned in spikes**. Online: genuine multi-layer spike-based learning — lower accuracy, no result beyond MNIST, and the strongest entries (99.05%) are layer-wise local training with a hand-crafted difference-of-Gaussian front end and an SVM readout.
